Our model is built from the qc objects
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{L\kappa}}^{\mathrm{qc}}
in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{L\kappa}}
. The
construction of Section
7.B
shows that the left adjoint

\ell
induces a left adjoint

\ell_{*}:\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{qc}}=\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{qc}}(L_{\mathfrak{g}}\pi)\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{L\kappa}}^{\mathrm{qc}}(L_{\mathfrak{g}}\pi\ell)=\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{L\kappa}}^{\mathrm{qc}}.

8.
The cellular skeletons

We have explained that given a coreflective inclusion
i:\mathcal{S}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}
of a subcategory, then if
\mathcal{C}
has a cofibrantly generated model
structure with generators in
\mathcal{S}
then by
[
22
]
the
inclusion is an equivalence. To fill in the vertical equivalences in
the roadmap of Section
5
it remains only to construct
the right adjoints and observe the generators come from the smaller
subcategories.

8.A.
Quasi-coherification

The quasi-coherification

\Gamma_{qc}X
of an object

\displaystyle X=\left[\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 7.56248pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 31.56248pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 38.59026pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern-7.56248pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.63748pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 31.63748pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{Q}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}\right]

As before, a solid bullet denotes an isomorphism after base change. In more detail, the dotted arrow from
N
to
Q
means we have a map

L_{\mathfrak{g}}N\longrightarrow Q
and we obtain
V^{\prime}
by pulling back along it. The
object
\Gamma_{\mathrm{qc}}
is highlighted in red.

The two-coloured display in the definition shows the counit
i\Gamma_{\mathrm{qc}}X\longrightarrow X
. It is also obvious that if
X
is qc then
\Gamma_{\mathrm{qc}}iX=X
. The
triangular identities are immediate, so that we have an adjunction

\textstyle{\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{qc}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Gamma_{\mathrm{qc}}}

The definition of
\Gamma_{\mathrm{qc}}
on
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{L\kappa}}
is exactly similar,
and it is easy to see they are also right adjoint to the
inclusion. This gives the bottom half of the road map in Subsection
5.B
.

is defined as a pullback and

is

. It is also obvious that if

. The

\textstyle{\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}^{e}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

8.C.
The quasi-coherification extendification functor

In order to construct an adjoint to the inclusion
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{qce}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{qc}}
we need an additional condition.

The Bousfield localization
L_{\mathfrak{g}}
is smashing in the sense that

L_{\mathfrak{g}}X\simeq L_{\mathfrak{g}}\mathbbm{1}\otimes X
, and it follows that multiplication gives a weak
equivalence
L_{\mathfrak{g}}\mathbbm{1}\otimes L_{\mathfrak{g}}\mathbbm{1}\simeq L_{\mathfrak{g}}\mathbbm{1}
. When forming
algebraic models, we may obtain a leaner model by requiring this to hold more
strictly.

Definition 8.1
.

We say that a localization functor
L
is
strictly smashing
if
the multiplication map
L\mathbbm{1}\otimes L\mathbbm{1}\longrightarrow L\mathbbm{1}
is an
isomorphism and
L\mathbbm{1}
is flat.

Example 8.2
.

(i)

If
\mathcal{C}
is the category of (chain complexes of)
R
-modules for a commutative ring
R
and

L
is localization to invert a multiplicatively closed set, then
L

is strictly smashing.

(ii)

If
\mathcal{C}
is the category of (chain complexes of) quasi-coherent sheaves on a curve
C

the generic localization

L_{\mathfrak{g}}
is passage to stalks over the generic point, and this is
also strictly smashing.

Definition 8.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a 1-dimensional Noetherian model category. We will say that
\mathcal{C}
has
strict generization
if
L_{\mathfrak{g}}
is strictly smashing.

All the examples we have in mind are based on categories of
differential graded modules, so they are simplicial and have strict
generization essentially as in Example
8.2
.

Lemma 8.4
.

If we have strict generization, then the functor
\Gamma_{e}
preserves
\mathrm{qc}
-modules. Hence

\Gamma_{\mathrm{qce}}=\Gamma_{e}\Gamma_{\mathrm{qc}}
is the right adjoint to the inclusion
i:\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{qce}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}
.

Proof.

It is clear that
\Gamma_{\mathrm{qc}}
lands in the category where the horizontal base change map is an isomorphism. As such, it is enough to assume that we have
X\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{qc}}
and that applying
\Gamma_{\mathrm{e}}
preserves the property of being
\mathrm{qc}
. We set

X=\left[\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 7.56248pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 37.74304pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 44.77081pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern-7.56248pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.16249pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.6pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\text{\Large\textbullet}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.56248pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 31.56248pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{L_{\mathfrak{g}}N}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}\right]

Then

\Gamma_{\mathrm{qce}}X=\Gamma_{\mathrm{e}}X=\left[\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 40.77359pt\raise-14.92113pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.6pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\text{\Large\textbullet}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.17358pt\raise-24.34225pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise We need to show that
L_{\mathfrak{g}}N^{\prime}\cong j_{\ast}V
, but this follows
from the fact that
L_{\mathfrak{g}}
is strictly smashing:
we apply
L_{\mathfrak{g}}
to the diagram to view it in the category of
L_{\mathfrak{g}}\mathbbm{1}
-modules and note that pullbacks preserve isomorphisms.
∎

8.D.
The idempotent extendification functor

The remaining torsion functor is a little more complicated.

Lemma 8.5
.

The inclusion
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{ie}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}
has a right adjoint
\Gamma_{\mathrm{ie}}:\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{ie}
, and if we have strict generization,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{ie}}
restricts to a functor

\Gamma_{\mathrm{ie}}:\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{qc}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{qcie}}
right adjoint to the inclusion

\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{qcie}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{qc}}
.

Proof.

We suppose given an object
X
as above and define

\Gamma_{\mathrm{ie}}X=\left[\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 20.46532pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 49.10918pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 56.06819pt\raise-24.34225pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern-20.46532pt\raise-31.67557pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\{M^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{m}}\}_{\mathfrak{m}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 44.46532pt\raise-31.67557pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 44.46532pt\raise-31.67557pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{j_{*}U}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}\right]

where the factors
M^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{m}}
are defined by the diagram

\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{j_{*}U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\prod_{\mathfrak{m}}M_{\mathfrak{m}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\prod_{\mathfrak{m}}M^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{m}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{P^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\prod_{\mathfrak{m}}M_{\mathfrak{m}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P}

where
M^{\prime}
is a pullback of
j_{*}U
and
\prod_{\mathfrak{m}}M_{\mathfrak{m}}

and

M^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{m}}=e_{\mathfrak{m}}M^{\prime}
, and
P^{\prime}
is defined as a pushout.
The pullback and pushout are taken in
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{m}}\mathbbm{1}
-modules,
with
P^{\prime}
local because
L_{\mathfrak{g}}
preserves pushouts.
Because the back left hand vertical is ie, so is the
lower back left hand vertical, so
\Gamma_{\mathrm{ie}}X
is indeed idempotent extended.
It is clear that
\Gamma_{\mathrm{ie}}
is the identity on idempotent extended
objects, so the unit of the adjunction is the identity. The counit

i\Gamma_{\mathrm{ie}}X\longrightarrow X
is displayed in the diagram above, and the triangular
identities are easily checked.

Furthermore, if the base change of
\alpha
is an isomorphism, it
follows that the base change of
\beta
is an isomorphism
by and hence that the base change of
\gamma
is an isomorphism.
∎

9.
The skeletal Quillen equivalences

Referring to the categories in the Roadmap (Subsection

5.B
), we first need to consider the existence of model
structures, and then establish the Quillen equivalences.

9.A.
Cellular skeletons of the adelic model

By Lemma
8.4
,
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{qce}}
is a coreflective
subcategory of the cofibrantly generated model category
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}
. To
see that it admits a left-lifted model
structure it suffices to show the generating cofibrations and acyclic cofibrations
are also
\mathrm{qce}
objects
[
22
]
.

Lemma 9.1
.

For a weakly qce object
X
, the counit
\Gamma_{\mathrm{qce}}X\xrightarrow{\simeq}X
is an equivalence in
R_{\mathrm{wqce}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}
, so
cellularization with respect to
\mathrm{wqce}
coincides with that for

\mathrm{qce}
.

For a weakly qc object
X
, the counit
\Gamma_{\mathrm{qc}}X\xrightarrow{\simeq}X
is an equivalence in
R_{\mathrm{wqc}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}
, so
cellularization with respect to
\mathrm{wqc}
coincides with that for
\mathrm{qc}
.

We will provide a chain of weak equivalences

. We start by fixing our object of interest

There is no change at the

is assumed to be proper.

3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}N^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 15.63222pt\raise-32.01782pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.6pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\text{\Large\textbullet}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 33.09917pt\raise-32.01782pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-56.36008pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 33.09917pt\raise-32.01782pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}j_{\ast}V^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 42.57358pt\raise-47.91116pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.20001pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\text{\Large$\circ$}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.17358pt\raise-56.36008pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern-7.56248pt\raise-63.69339pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.86388pt\raise-63.69339pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.6pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\text{\Large\textbullet}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 32.96526pt\raise-63.69339pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 32.96526pt\raise-63.69339pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{L_{\mathfrak{g}}N}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}\right]

There is no change at the
V^{\prime}
vertex, so we need to check at the other two points.
We must show the maps
N^{\prime}\to N
and
j_{\ast}V^{\prime}\to L_{\mathfrak{g}}N
are
weak equivalences. As the
\Gamma_{\mathrm{qc}}(X)
is weakly extended, we
have that
j_{\ast}V^{\prime}\to L_{\mathfrak{g}}N
is a weak equivalence. It then
follows that
N^{\prime}\to N
is also a weak equivalence as we are pulling
back along an isomorphism.
∎

Corollary 9.2

(Adelic Cellular Skeleton Theorem)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a 1-dimensional Noetherian model category admitting
strict generization. Then inclusions give Quillen equivalences

\mathcal{C}\simeq_{Q}R_{\mathrm{wqce}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}\simeq_{Q}R_{\mathrm{we}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{qc}}\simeq_{Q}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{qce}}.

We refer to
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{qce}}
as the cellular skeleton of the adelic model.

Proof.

The adelic model is Theorem
3.2
.
By Subsection
8.A
Lemma
8.4
,
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{qc}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{qce}}

are coreflective subcategories of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}
. By Lemma
9.1

\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{qc}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{qce}}
have model structures
left-lifted from
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}
. By
[
22
]
these are cellular skeletons
∎

9.B.
Model structures on qc-diagram categories

We consider model categories in the context of the types of categories appearing in Subsections
7.A
and
7.B
.
Suppose
\mathcal{N},\mathcal{V},\mathcal{Q}
are cofibrantly
generated model categories. We give the diagram category
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}
the diagram-injective model
structure, cellularize at
R_{\mathrm{wqc}}
, and give
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}^{qc}
the left-lifted model structure.

Since
hF^{r}
is not a left adjoint, it is not clear that the entire category of sections
\mathcal{C}_{F_{*}\alpha}
admits a
diagram-injective model structure. The main point of this subsection is to observe that the subcategory
\mathcal{C}_{F_{*}\alpha}^{qc}
of quasi-coherent objects does admit a model structure
right-lifted along
F^{*}
from
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}^{qc}
.

Lemma 9.3
.

If
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
has cylinders, admits a model structure right-lifted along
F^{r}
from

\mathcal{N}
, and the functor
v
is faithfully flat, then
\mathcal{C}_{F_{*}\alpha}^{qc}
admits a model structure right-lifted along
F^{*}
from

\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}^{qc}
. In this model structure cofibrant objects have cofibrant
nubs and fibrant objects have fibrant nubs.

Proof.

For an object
N
of
\mathcal{N}
we consider the cospan
a(N)=(N\longrightarrow hN\longleftarrow 0)
. The functor
a
is left adjoint to evaluation:

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}^{qc}}(a(N),X)=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{N}}(N,N_{X}).

For an object
U
of
\mathcal{V}
we consider the cospan,

b(U)=(0\longrightarrow 0\longleftarrow U)
, also left adjoint

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}^{qc}}(b(U),X)=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{V}}(U,fV_{X}),

but now to the vertex-fibre:
fV_{X}
is the subobject of

V_{X}
mapping to
\ker(vV_{X}\longrightarrow hN_{X})
.

Suppose that
I_{\mathcal{N}}
and
J_{\mathcal{N}}
are the sets of generating
cofibrations and acyclic cofibrations for
\mathcal{N}
and similarly for

\mathcal{V}
. Take
I=\{a(N),b(U)\;|\;N\in I_{\mathcal{N}},U\in I_{\mathcal{V}}\}
, and similarly for
J
. We then see that
I-inj
consists of
morphisms with nub component in
I_{\mathcal{N}}-inj
and vertex components
with
fV_{X}
in
I_{\mathcal{V}}-inj
. The adjunctions mean that maps out of maps
in
I
or
J
are detected either in the nub or in the vertex-fibre.

Now consider the requirements of
[
24
, 2.1.19]
. Conditions 1,
2, and 3 are obvious. Condition 4 is also obvious by the adjunctions since these
conditions also hold at vertex and nub. Similarly for Condition 6. For Condition 5,
I-inj\subseteq J-inj
is clear, and it remains to comment on
I-inj\subseteq\mathcal{W}
. Using the objects
a(N)
we see that a map in
I-inj
is an
equivalence at the nub. From the strict smashing condition it follows
that it is an equivalence also at the adelic vertex. Finally, using
the objects
b(U)
we see that it is an equivalence at the
vertex-fibre. It remains to deduce that it is an equivalence at the
vertex. Since
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
has cyclinders, this can be done with acyclics. We have a pullback square

\textstyle{fV\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{vV\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{hN}

Now we know that
hN
and
fV
are acyclic, so it follows that
hV
is
acyclic. Since
v
is faithfully flat, it follows that
V
is acyclic as required.
∎

Finally, we observe that with this model structure, if the left adjoint
F:\mathcal{N}\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
is a
Quillen equivalence then the left adjoint
F_{*}:\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}^{qc}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{F_{*}\alpha}^{qc}
is also
a Quillen equivalence.

Suppose
X
is a cofibrant object of
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}
and
Y
is a fibrant
object of
\mathcal{C}_{F_{*}\alpha}
we must show that
F_{*}X\longrightarrow Y
is a weak
equivalence if and only if its adjunct
X\longrightarrow F^{*}Y
is a weak
equivalence. However
F_{*}
and
F^{*}
do not change the vertex, and at
the nub they are
FN\longrightarrow N^{\prime}
and
N\longrightarrow F^{r}N^{\prime}
: since a cofibrant
object of
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}^{qc}
has cofibrant nub and a fibrant object in

\mathcal{C}_{F_{*}\alpha}^{qc}
has fibrant nub, the result follows.

9.C.
The skeleton of the separated model

We have shown in Subsection
8.A
and Lemma
8.5
that

\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{qc}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{qcie}}

are coreflective inclusions.

Lemma 9.4
.

(i)

For a
\mathrm{wqcie}
object
X
, the counit
\Gamma_{\mathrm{qcie}}X\xrightarrow{\simeq}X
is an equivalence in
R_{\mathrm{wqcie}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}
, so
cellularization with respect to
\mathrm{wqcie}
coincides with that for

\mathrm{qcie}
.

(ii)

For a
\mathrm{wie}

\mathrm{qc}
-object
X
, the counit
\Gamma_{\mathrm{ie}}X\xrightarrow{\simeq}X
is an equivalence in
R_{\mathrm{wie}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{qc}}
, so
cellularization with respect to
\mathrm{wie}
coincides with that for

\mathrm{ie}
.

(iii)

For a weakly qc object
X
, the counit
\Gamma_{\mathrm{qc}}X\xrightarrow{\simeq}X
is an equivalence in
R_{\mathrm{wqc}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}
, so
cellularization with respect to
\mathrm{wqc}
coincides with that for
\mathrm{qc}
.

Proof.

The proof of Part (iii) is exactly like the corresponding part in the
adelic case.

For Part (i) we consider the last diagram of Lemma
8.5
, and
repeatedly use left and right properness. The notable point is that
the map
M^{\prime}\longrightarrow\prod_{\mathfrak{m}}M_{\mathfrak{m}}
is only an idempotent weak
equivalence, but of course this
implies that
\prod_{\mathfrak{m}}M_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}\longrightarrow\prod_{\mathfrak{m}}M_{\mathfrak{m}}
is an actual weak
equivalence.

The argument for Part (ii) is similar.
∎

Theorem 9.5
.

(Separated Skeleton Theorem)
Let
\mathcal{C}
be a 1-dimensional Noetherian model category admitting
strict generization, and suppose
v
is faithfully flat. Then there are Quillen equivalences

\mathcal{C}\simeq_{Q}L_{\Pi}R_{\mathrm{wqcie}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}=L_{\Pi}R_{\mathrm{wie}}R_{\mathrm{wqc}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}\simeq_{Q}L_{\Pi}R_{\mathrm{wie}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{qc}}\simeq_{Q}R_{\mathrm{wie}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{qc}}\simeq_{Q}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{qcie}}

and hence the skeleton of the separated model
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{qcie}}
is Quillen
equivalent to
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.1
,
\mathcal{C}
is equivalent to the separated model
L_{\Pi}R_{\mathrm{wqcie}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}
.
The equivalence
L_{\Pi}R_{\mathrm{wie}}R_{\mathrm{wqc}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}\simeq L_{\Pi}R_{\mathrm{wie}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{qc}}
follows from the fact that
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{qc}}
is a cellular skeleton
of
R_{\mathrm{wqc}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}
(Corollary
9.2
). The equivalence
R_{\mathrm{wie}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{qc}}\simeq\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{qcie}}
follows from Lemma
9.4
since the objects of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{ad}}
are in the image of the
reflective inclusion
\sigma^{*}
.

The equivalence
L_{\Pi}R_{\mathrm{wie}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{qc}}\simeq L_{\Pi}R_{\mathrm{wie}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{qc}}
is
based on the adjoints from Subsection
7.C
.
Subsection
9.B
shows that
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{qc}}
admits a model
structure right-lifted from
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{qc}}
. Accordingly, the
\sigma_{*}\dashv\sigma^{*}
adjunction is
Quillen for the model structures on
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{qc}}
and

\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{qc}}
. Cellularizing both sides, it remains
Quillen for the model structures on
R_{\mathrm{we}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{qc}}
and

R_{\mathrm{wie}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{qc}}
. Finally, it remains a Quillen adjunction relating
L_{\Pi}R_{\mathrm{we}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{qc}}
and
R_{\mathrm{wie}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{qc}}
, and this adjunction is a
Quillen equivalence from the definition.
∎

Remark 9.6
.

We may consider the following ‘super-separated’ diagram

\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ssep}}={\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}}\left(\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise where the horizontal functor is localization
L_{\mathfrak{g}}
on each
factor, and the vertical functor is extension of scalars on each
factor. However, this cannot form a model for
\mathcal{C}
as we have simply removed too much
information: the splicing data for assembling the contributions at
individual closed points needs to be ‘the same almost everywhere’ in
the sense that an element of the adeles is given by
(x_{p})\in\prod_{p}\mathbb{Q}_{p}
with
x_{p}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\wedge}
for almost all
p
.

From another point of view the super-separated square
fails to be a model because the square

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern to0.0pt{.\hss}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

is usually not a homotopy pullback square.

9.D.
The skeleton of the complete model

Under somewhat more restrictive hypotheses, we identify a local skeleton of the complete model
L_{\Lambda}R_{\mathrm{wqc}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}
.

By the same proof as Lemma
9.1
we compare weak
and strong
\mathrm{qc}
objects.

Lemma 9.7
.

For a weakly qc object
X
in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{L\kappa}}
, the counit
\Gamma_{\mathrm{qc}}X\xrightarrow{\simeq}X
is an equivalence in
R_{\mathrm{wqc}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{L\kappa}}
, so
cellularization with respect to
\mathrm{wqc}
coincides with that for
\mathrm{qc}
. ∎

Theorem 9.8
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a 1-dimensional Noetherian model category which is
formally algebraic, has strict generization and so that
v
is
faithfully flat. Then there are Quillen equivalences

\mathcal{C}\simeq_{Q}R_{\mathrm{wqce}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}\simeq_{Q}L_{\Lambda}L_{\Pi}R_{\mathrm{wqc}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}\simeq_{Q}L_{\Lambda}L_{\Pi}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{qc}}\simeq_{Q}L_{\Lambda}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{qc}}\simeq_{Q}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{L\kappa}}^{\mathrm{qc}}.

and hence the skeleton of the complete model
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{L\kappa}}^{\mathrm{qc}}
is Quillen
equivalent to
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

The equivalences up to
L_{\Lambda}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{qc}}
are exactly as in Theorem

9.5
.
For the remaining equivalence
L_{\Lambda}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{qc}}\simeq\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{L\kappa}}^{\mathrm{qc}}
,
we combine the left adjoints
L_{0}^{\mathfrak{m}}:R_{\mathfrak{m}}\text{-mod}\longrightarrow L_{0}^{\mathfrak{m}}-R_{\mathfrak{m}}\text{-mod}
, to obtain

\ell:\prod_{\mathfrak{m}}(R_{\mathfrak{m}}\text{-mod})\longrightarrow\prod_{\mathfrak{m}}(L_{0}^{\mathfrak{m}}-R_{\mathfrak{m}}\text{-mod})

and then use the construction of Section

7.B
to give a functor
\ell_{*}\colon\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{qc}}\to\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{L\kappa}}^{\mathrm{qc}}

left adjoint to the inclusion. We give
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{L\kappa}}^{\mathrm{qc}}
the right-lifted model
structure as in Subsection
9.B
, giving a Quillen
adjunction relating
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{qc}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{L\kappa}}^{\mathrm{qc}}
. This localizes further
to give a Quillen
adjunction relating
L_{\Lambda}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{qc}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{L\kappa}}^{\mathrm{qc}}
, and this is a
Quillen equivalence by applying Proposition
6.3
at the nub.
∎

Part III
Examples

In the final part of this paper where we make our results explicit in several
examples, aiming to give a systematic and connected account easing comparison.

10.
Abelian groups

The prototypical example is the derived category of abelian groups

\operatorname{Ho}(\mathcal{C})=\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{Z})
. This applies without change
if
\mathbb{Z}
is replaced by an arbitrary 1-dimensional commutative
Noetherian domain.

The underlying category is
\mathbb{Z}\text{-mod}
. Here
\mathbb{Z}
is viewed
as a dg ring entirely in degree 0 with zero differential. Thus
\mathbb{Z}\text{-mod}
means
the category of dg
\mathbb{Z}
-modules (i.e., chain complexes of abelian
groups). (When we need to consider abelian groups without grading or
differential, we say so explicitly).

We make
\mathbb{Z}\text{-mod}
into a model category using
the (algebraically) projective model structure.
There is a natural order-reversing homeomorphism

\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{Spc}^{\omega}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{Z}))

where the classical
algebraic
prime
\mathfrak{p}
corresponds to the

Balmer
prime
\mathfrak{p}_{b}=\{M\;|\;M_{\mathfrak{p}}\simeq 0\}

[
5
]
. The closed points are the algebraically maximal primes
(p)
, and there is a unique generic point corresponding to the algebraic prime
(0)
, and we write
\mathbb{Q}=\mathbb{Z}_{(0)}
for the field of fractions.

The adelic approximation theorem gives a homotopy pullback square in
\mathbb{Z}\text{-mod}
:

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern We note that this square is also a strict pullback square in the
underlying category of abelian groups, familiar as the classical Hasse square.

In this setting, we have both strict generization and
p
-adic
completion functors, so we will get three different skeletons.

9.2
, the cellular skeleton of the adelic model is

Remark 10.1
.

To give a feeling for
the different models, we recommend that the
reader identifies the images in the adelic, separated
and complete models of

\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{Z})
for the abelian groups
\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z},\bigoplus_{p}\mathbb{Z}/p,\prod_{p}\mathbb{Z}/p
and moreover explores moving between
the various models. For example the abelian group
\mathbb{Q}
corresponds to
the diagrams

\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}\otimes\prod_{p}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\wedge}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}\otimes\prod_{p}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\wedge}}
\textstyle{\{\mathbb{Q}_{p}\}_{p}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\prod_{p}\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

in the adelic, separated and complete modules respectively. Similarly
the abelian group
\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
corresponds to
the diagrams

\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{p}\mathbb{Z}/p^{\infty}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{\{\mathbb{Z}/p^{\infty}\}_{p}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}\otimes\prod_{p}\mathbb{Z}/p^{\infty}}
\textstyle{\{\Sigma\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\wedge}\}_{p}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}\otimes\prod_{p}\Sigma\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\wedge}}

in the adelic, separated and complete modules respectively.

Remark 10.2
.

In this case all of the rings appearing are commutative, and the equivalence is monoidal, so we may wonder about invertible modules. Over
\mathbb{Z}
, invertible modules are suspensions of
\mathbb{Z}
, but when
\mathbb{Z}
is
replaced by a number ring
R
, this is more interesting:
if
M
is an invertible module
M_{(0)}\cong R_{(0)}
,
and
M_{\mathfrak{p}}\cong R_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\wedge}
so
that the module is specified by non-zero elements
x_{\mathfrak{p}}\in R_{(0)}\otimes R_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\wedge}
for each closed point
\mathfrak{p}
. The module is isomorphic to
R
if

(x_{\mathfrak{p}})
comes from
R_{(0)}
, so invertible modules correspond to

[R_{(0)}\otimes\prod_{\mathfrak{p}}R_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\wedge}]^{\times}/R_{(0)}^{\times}
in
a familiar way.

11.
Quasi-coherent sheaves on a curve

Let
C
be an irreducible curve over an algebraically closed field
k
with
structure sheaf
\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}_{C}
, and ring of rational functions
\mathcal{K}
. We
take
\mathcal{C}=\textbf{QCoh}(C)
to be the category of (complexes of) sheaves of
quasi-coherent modules over
C
. The
Balmer spectrum consists of the points of
C
, with a single generic
point containing all the closed points.
The Adelic Approximation Theorem states that we have a homotopy pullback
of the diagram

\left(\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 17.07295pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.8889pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{K}\otimes\prod_{x}\mathcal{O}_{x}^{\wedge}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}\right)

The category
\textbf{QCoh}(C)
of (chain complexes of)
quasi-coherent sheaves of modules has strict generization, so we may
form the skeleton of the separated model for
\textbf{QCoh}(C)
. This
is the category

\textbf{QCoh}(C)_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{qcie}}={\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}}\left(\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 28.32298pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 71.39592pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 52.32298pt\raise-31.73112pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 52.32298pt\raise-31.73112pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{K}\otimes\prod_{x}\mathcal{O}_{x}^{\wedge}\text{-mod}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}\right)

where the product is over closed points
x
. We do not make the adelic
or complete models explicit.

Finally, we note the connection between the Adelic Approximation
Theorem and cohomology of line bundles.
Tensoring the pullback square for
\mathcal{O}
with the line bundle
\mathcal{O}(D)
of a divisor
D=\sum_{x}n_{x}(x)
and taking global sections, there is an exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{0}(C;\mathcal{O}(D))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{K}\times\prod_{x}\mathcal{O}_{x}^{\wedge}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle i,t_{x}^{-n_{x}}\rangle}
\textstyle{\mathcal{K}\otimes\prod_{x}\mathcal{O}_{x}^{\wedge}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}(C;\mathcal{O}(D))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

where
t_{x}
is a function vanishing to first order at
x
. This
recovers Weil’s residue approach to calculating cohomology as
explained in
[
30
, II.5]

Finally, we note that the Adelic Approximation Square embodies the
comparison between the Cousin and residue complexes: the top row
gives the Cousin complex

\mathbbm{1}\longrightarrow L_{\mathfrak{g}}\mathbbm{1}\longrightarrow[L_{\mathfrak{g}}\mathbbm{1}]/\mathbbm{1}

(which would be
\mathbb{Z}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Q}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
for abelian groups)
and the Mayer-Vietoris sequence of the Hasse-Tate square is Weil’s
residue complex as above (it would be
\mathbb{Z}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Q}\oplus\prod_{p}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\wedge}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Q}\otimes\prod_{p}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\wedge}
for abelian groups).

12.
Stable modules for the Klein 4-group

Our next example comes from modular representation theory. Let

G
be a finite group, and
k
a field with characteristic
dividing the order of
G
. We consider the category of

kG
-modules. Say that two morphisms
f,g\colon M\to N
are
stably homotopic
if
f-g\colon M\to N
factors through a
projective module. The category of
kG
-modules has a model
structure in which the weak equivalences are the stable
equivalences, the fibrations are the epimorphisms and the
cofibrations are the monomorphisms
[
24
, Theorem 2.2.12]
. The homotopy category of this
model category is the (big) stable module category

\underline{\text{Mod}}kG
. Passage to syzygies gives the
desuspension functor in the stable module category. Tensor product
over
k
equips the stable module category with the structure of a
rigidly small-generated tensor-triangulated category with monoidal unit
k
.

The Balmer spectrum of the small objects is the projective scheme associated to the group
cohomology ring
[
12
]
:

\mathrm{Spc}^{\omega}(\underline{\text{Mod}}kG)\cong\operatorname{Proj}H^{\bullet}(G;k).

In particular, since
H^{\bullet}(G;k)
is a Noetherian graded ring by
Venkov’s Theorem, the Balmer spectrum is Noetherian.

When
G=E
is the Klein 4-group
E=\langle g,h\mid g^{2}=h^{2}=1,gh=hg\rangle
and
k
is an algebraically closed field of
characteristic 2, the cohomology ring
H^{*}(E;k)=\mathrm{Ext}_{kE}^{*}(k,k)=k[u,v]
is easy to deal with and
the representation theory is very well understood. We take

x=g-1
and
y=h-1
and note
kE\cong k[x,y]/(x^{2},y^{2})
. From this
point of view, the stable module category of
kE
is the singularity category of

k[x,y]/(x^{2},y^{2})
and the BGG correspondence
[
11
]

states that the singularity category is equivalent to the bounded derived category of

\mathbb{P}^{1}(k)=\mathrm{Proj}(k[u,v])
, giving another identification of the Balmer spectrum.

It is helpful to display
kE
as

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces x}
\textstyle{y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{xy}

where a single line refers to multiplication by
x
and a double line
refers to multiplication by
y
.
One sees syzygies are as follows

The labels refer to the fact that
H^{n}(E)=\mathrm{Hom}(\Omega^{n}k,k)
is the dual of the top
vector space in the diagram: we have chosen the pairing between
k[u,v]
and

k[u^{-1},v^{-1}]
by multiplying and finding the constant term.
Of course
H^{*}(E)=\mathrm{Symm(E^{*})}
, and
\{u,v\}
is the dual basis to

\{x,y\}
. Similarly

The labels refer to the fact that
H^{n}(E)=\mathrm{Hom}(k,\Omega^{-n}k)
is
canonically isomorphic to the bottom row in the diagram. This type of
labelling applies to general modules. In the stable module category, any module is equivalent to one
with no free summands, and for such an
M
, we see that
(xy)=(x,y)^{2}

acts as zero. We therefore write
V_{l}=\mathrm{ann}_{M}(x,y)
(
l
for
‘lower’) for the socle, and we may choose a complementary subspace

V_{u}
(
u
for ‘upper’). Multiplication by
x,y
gives
two maps
x,y:V_{u}\longrightarrow V_{l}
. We also have a canonical isomorphism

[k,M]_{0}=V_{l}
. The group
[k,M]_{*}
has the structure of a module over

[k,k]^{*}=\hat{H}^{*}(E)
, and
V_{l}
is its degree 0 part.
We will constantly be passing between degree 0
information and the full graded module, and it is essential to keep
clear which of the worlds each statement lives in.
The syzygies of
k
have the property that there are endomorphisms

t:V_{u}\longrightarrow V_{u}
and
t:V_{l}\longrightarrow V_{l}
with
y=xt
and
tx=y
as
maps
V_{u}\longrightarrow V_{l}
, and it is natural to think as if
t=v/u
.

We will also need another family of modules. For

\zeta\in H^{n}(G,k)=\mathrm{Hom}_{kG}(\Omega^{n}k,k)
, we may take

\mathbb{L}_{\zeta}=\ker(\zeta:\Omega^{n}k\longrightarrow k)
, which only depends on

\zeta
up to scalar multiplication. Since
k
is algebraically
closed, all homogeneous elements of
H^{\ast}(E,k)=k[u,v]
are products of
degree 1 elements, so we need only consider
\mathbb{L}_{\lambda u+\mu v}
for

[\lambda:\mu]\in\mathbb{P}^{1}(k)
. The module
k
has support
\mathbb{P}^{1}(k)
and
for closed points
\zeta
, the module
\mathbb{L}_{\zeta}
has support

\{\zeta\}
.

Nullifying
\mathbb{L}_{\zeta}
has the effect of inverting
\zeta
in
homotopy. Now
[k,k]_{*}=\hat{H}^{*}(E;k)
, and the norm sequence of

H^{*}(E)
-modules is the short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow H^{*}(E)\longrightarrow\hat{H}^{*}(E)\longrightarrow\Sigma_{-1}H_{*}(E)\longrightarrow 0,

where
\Sigma_{n}
is the cohomological suspension

(\Sigma_{n}M)^{c}=M^{c-n}
. Since
H_{*}(E)
is torsion,
this shows that inverting
\zeta
makes
H^{*}(E)\longrightarrow\hat{H}^{*}(E)
into
an isomorphism. Localizing at the prime
(\zeta)
involves nullifying
all
\mathbb{L}_{\psi}
with
\psi\neq\zeta
and the generic localization

L_{\mathfrak{g}}k
involves nullifying all
\mathbb{L}_{\zeta}

Thus

[k,L_{\zeta}k]_{*}=k[u,v]_{(\zeta)},[k,L_{\mathfrak{g}}k]_{*}=k[u,v]_{(0)}.

Note here that
k[u,v]_{(0)}
is
k(t)
in degree 0 with
t=v/u
and
any non-zero element
\zeta\in H^{1}(E)
is a periodicity
element. Similarly in
k[u,v]_{(\zeta)}
, except that the periodicity
element is any element not a multiple of
\zeta
.

In fact we can see in both cases how to realize these modules, by
taking the direct limit along multiples of maps
\zeta\in H^{1}(E)
. It
is convenient to think of
\zeta:k\longrightarrow\Omega^{-1}k
and so forth, so
using the identification of the lower module with the homotopy, we have

L_{\mathfrak{g}}k=\left(\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}}{\hbox{\kern-13.11108pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{k(t)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}\right)

Of course it is natural to think of
k(t)
the ring of rational functions on
\mathbb{P}^{1}(k)
, and similarly, for a closed point
\zeta\in\mathbb{P}^{1}(k)
we can describe
L_{\zeta}k
using the rational functions
regular at
\zeta
.

L_{\zeta}k=\left(\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{k[u,v]_{(\zeta)}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}\right)

Next, we may calculate the derived completions in general by the formula

\Lambda_{\zeta}M=\mathop{\mathop{\mathrm{holim}}\limits_{\leftarrow}}\nolimits_{s}M/\zeta^{s}.

Picking
\zeta=u
for definiteness, one finds

[k,\Lambda_{u}k]_{*}=k[u,v,v^{-1}]_{u}^{\wedge},

where the inversion of
v
could be replaced by the inversion of any
other degree 1 element (except for multiples of

u
). We note that the degree 0 part of this is the completed stalk
of the point
\infty
(defined by
u=0
):

[k,\Lambda_{u}k]_{0}=\left[\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}(k)}\right]_{\infty}^{\wedge}.

Continuing to the completion we want, we have the usual splitting

(L_{\mathfrak{g}}k)/k\simeq\bigoplus_{\zeta}(k[1/\zeta])/k

and hence

\Lambda_{0}M:=\mathrm{Hom}(L_{\mathfrak{g}}k/k,M)\simeq\prod_{\zeta}\Lambda_{\zeta}M.

The Adelic Approximation Theorem states that there is a homotopy pullback in the category
\underline{\text{Mod}}(kE)

\textstyle{k\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L_{\mathfrak{g}}k\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Lambda_{0}k\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L_{\mathfrak{g}}\Lambda_{0}k}

A localization of this is considered in relation to the Chromatic
Splitting Conjecture in
[
8
, Example 3.18]
.

Applying
[k,\cdot]_{*}
we obtain the square

\textstyle{\hat{H}^{*}(E)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{k(t)[u,u^{-1}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\prod_{\zeta}k[u,v,(\zeta^{\prime})^{-1}]_{\zeta}^{\wedge}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\prod_{\zeta}k[u,v,(\zeta^{\prime})^{-1}]_{\zeta}^{\wedge})_{(0)}}

where
\zeta^{\prime}
is any element of
H^{1}(E)
not a multiple of
\zeta
.
The associated Mayer-Vietoris sequence of this square is the residue complex for
calculating the cohomology of
\mathbb{P}^{1}(k)
with coefficients in twists
of the structure sheaf as in Section
11
.

One can then use the above splitting to build an adelic, separated and
complete model.

13.
\mathbb{T}
-equivariant rational stable
homotopy theory

We finish the examples with a discussion of the case that motivated
this work, namely the category of

\mathbb{T}
-equivariant rational cohomology theories, where
\mathbb{T}
is the
circle group.

For a general compact Lie group
G
, we say that an inclusion
K\subseteq H
of subgroups of
G
is
cotoral
if

K
is normal in
H
and
H/K
is a torus. The Balmer spectrum consists of the
conjugacy classes of subgroups under cotoral inclusion
[
16
]
.

In particular, the Balmer spectrum of
\textbf{Sp}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{\mathbb{T}}
is

This model is the first step in
[
21
]
to the algebraic model for rational

\mathbb{T}
-equivariant spectra. One then shows that taking
\mathbb{T}
-fixed points
termwise gives an equivalence, and the resulting ring spectra are
formal, with homotopy

\mathbb{S}^{alg}_{\mathrm{ad}}=\left(\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 19.27437pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 62.65291pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 69.54181pt\raise-23.34663pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-19.27437pt\raise-31.98666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\prod_{n}\mathbb{Q}[c]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 43.27437pt\raise-31.98666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 43.27437pt\raise-31.98666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{E}^{-1}\prod_{n}\mathbb{Q}[c]}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}\right)

We note that there is no single graded ring giving rise to this adelic
diagram, so the use of
diagrams
of rings is
essential. Taking modules over this diagram of rings,
we obtain the homotopical version of the standard algebraic model

The generic localization is inverting the set
\mathcal{E}
generated by the
Euler classes
e(z^{n})
(which is
c
at subgroups of
C_{n}
and 1
otherwise). It is therefore strictly smashing, so we can form skeletons of the
adelic, separated and complete models. The proof that the separated
and complete diagrams give models for rational

\mathbb{T}
-spectra appears here for the first time. The standard model is the
cellular skeleton of the algebraic model

dg\mathcal{A}(\mathbb{T})=(\mathbb{S}^{alg}_{\mathrm{ad}}\text{-mod})^{\mathrm{qce}}:={\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\displaystyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\textstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{L}$}}}}\left(\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise
